Peer punishment/reward (where all can punish/reward) have even larger effects. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. The marginal per capita return (MPCR) and group size also positively affect efficiency, but to a lesser extent. Leaders contribute more than followers, and followers contribute about 79% as much as leaders. Inequality within groups does not generally increase with leadership. Leaders' payoffs are similar to those of average players in simultaneous games. The paper provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes for each moderator.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games, conditional on game design dimensions. The meta-regressions include variables for group size, number of rounds, MPCR, presence and type of punishment/reward, and leadership structure. The results show that adding centralized punishment (by a leader) increases efficiency by about 0.26 (on a 0-1 scale of contributions relative to optimum), and peer punishment increases it by about 0.47. The effect of reward is positive but smaller. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. These findings can be used to adjust predicted efficiency when punishment is enabled, given the control efficiency and game design. The paper is highly relevant for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Xie, K., & Liu, T. J. (2024). The regulation of good and evi promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to both punish defectors and reward cooperators (PEPG strategy) in a spatial public goods game, especially when combined with heterogeneous payoff allocation based on reputation, leads to higher cooperation rates and higher average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the enhancement factor (r), the importance of reputation in payoff allocation (a), and the strength of the bonus-penalty (gamma). Defectors' average payoffs are always lower than those of cooperators in the steady state. The model demonstrates that the union of PEPG and reputation-based heterogeneity is more effective than either alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides simulation evidence that combining punishment and reward (PEPG) with reputation-based heterogeneous payoffs increases cooperation and average payoffs in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward and reputation mechanisms) will increase efficiency, particularly when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr, via r), the effectiveness of punishment/reward (gamma), and the importance of reputation (a) are high. However, the model is not a direct empirical test and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative optimum, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Ibrahimli, R., Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2024). Migration costs and rewarding schemes in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study simulates spatial public goods games where agents can migrate between groups if unsatisfied. Four setups are compared: baseline (migration only), migration cost (punishment), rewarding scheme (loyalty reward), and combined. Migration costs (punishment) robustly increase average contribution, especially at low enhancement factors, and are more effective than rewards. The combined mechanism can further improve cooperation in some cases. The main reported outcome is average contribution, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that institutional punishment (here, migration cost) is a stronger driver of cooperation than institutional rewards in this spatial setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, migration costs) increase cooperation (contribution) more than rewards in spatial PGGs, especially at low enhancement factors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding how migration costs and rewards affect contribution rates under different enhancement factors, group sizes, and migration probabilities. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Goto, J., & Matsui, T. (2025). Counterproductive punishment in closed communities: Experimental evidence from Japanese fishery. *Marine Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, before punishment is introduced, average contributions are similar in closed and open communities. After introducing the (indirect, weak) punishment mechanism, contributions decrease substantially in closed communities (by 17 percentage points) and increase slightly in open communities (by 4 percentage points). Regression analyses confirm that the negative effect in closed communities is robust to controls and alternative specifications. The mechanism appears to be that punishment in closed communities is perceived as excessive peer pressure, undermining trust and cooperative norms. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the direction of contribution changes suggests that punishment may reduce efficiency in closed communities and slightly increase it in open communities.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment depends on community structure: in environments with strong pre-existing cooperative norms and high internal communication (closed communities), even weak, indirect punishment can reduce contributions and likely efficiency. In more open communities, punishment has a small positive effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates. The punishment mechanism is indirect and low-cost, so results may not generalize to direct, costly punishment systems. The findings are most relevant for settings with similar social structure and weak, reputation-based punishment.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2022). The rise and fall of cooperation in populations with multiple groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of inequity-averse individuals (both punishers and rewarders) can promote the survival of contributors and the elimination of defectors in spatial public goods games, but the effectiveness depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost (alpha), and the intensity (beta) of punishment/reward. First-order inequity aversion (punishment) is more effective at low r, while second-order (reward) is more effective at high r. Increasing the cost of punishment/reward (alpha) undermines the effect of punishment more than reward. Increasing the intensity (beta) generally promotes cooperation, but can also lead to cyclic dominance phases where defectors persist. The model shows that the traditional result that punishment is more effective than reward does not always hold when punishment/reward is based on payoff inequity rather than strategy. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so implications for efficiency are indirect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and reward mechanisms, when based on payoff inequity rather than strategy, affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on the synergy factor, cost, and intensity, and that the relationship is non-monotonic and context-dependent. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding how design dimensions like mpcr (r), punishment/reward cost and effectiveness, and the presence of reward interact to affect the prevalence of cooperation, but not directly the efficiency.

- source: Yang, L. H., Ning, Y. P., & Zhang, L. Z. (2025). Reconstruction of micro-dynamics characterizing human decision-making behavior in repeated social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishing the minority strategy in each group (regardless of whether it is cooperation or defection) can strongly promote the emergence of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the punishment fine increases, the fraction of cooperators rises, and for high punishment, the system can abruptly transition from full defection to full cooperation as the multiplication factor increases. Theoretical analysis shows that the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors increases with punishment severity, favoring the expansion of cooperator clusters. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of this punishment rule on overall welfare or efficiency remains unclear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishing the minority strategy in a spatial PGG can increase the fraction of cooperators, especially with higher punishment fines. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. Theoretical payoff differences are provided, but without explicit efficiency or welfare analysis, it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates to higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment. The paper is most informative for predicting cooperation rates under minority punishment rules, but less so for efficiency predictions required by the downstream task.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. Y., Chen, W. M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Reputation-dependent social learning on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a conditional strategy—where agents can switch between excluding defectors and opting out as loners—substantially increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. Lower tolerance for defectors (i.e., agents become loners more readily), lower exclusion costs, and higher exclusion probability all lead to higher cooperation levels. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperative strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report efficiency as a fraction of the social optimum, nor does it provide direct evidence on how these mechanisms affect group payoff or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional exclusion and voluntary opt-out mechanisms can increase the frequency of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when exclusion is cheap and effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (social exclusion) are more effective when costs are low and effectiveness is high, but the translation to efficiency outcomes remains uncertain.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, M. Y., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2023). Combined effect of pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that social exclusion is a more effective strategy than punishment for promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. Introducing antisocial punishment (defective punishers) helps eliminate second-order free-riding by cooperators who do not bear exclusion costs. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the exclusion probability, antisocial punishment fine, and exclusion/punishment costs. High exclusion probability and low antisocial punishment fine promote cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperative excluders can protect cooperators from invasion by defectors and defective punishers, and that the combination of social exclusion and antisocial punishment can sustain high cooperation levels. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms like social exclusion and antisocial punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, which may be associated with higher efficiency in practice. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how the structure and parameters of punishment and exclusion mechanisms affect cooperation dynamics, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in similar game designs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results should be treated as informative about behavioral mechanisms but not as direct evidence.

- source: Liu, L. J., Xiao, Z. L., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Early exclusion leads to cyclical cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, the ability to exclude defectors early (after monitoring) leads to cyclical dominance among cooperators, defectors, and excluders, and maintains high average cooperation rates. If exclusion is delayed, defectors can dominate. The model shows that the timing of exclusion, monitoring cost, and discount factor are key moderators. While the model defines payoffs and uses them in evolutionary dynamics, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that enabling a form of punishment (social exclusion) can sustain high cooperation rates if implemented early, and that the timing and cost of exclusion are critical moderators. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as informative-indirect evidence. The model's structure and parameterization (group size, rounds, exclusion cost, monitoring cost) can inform how similar design dimensions might affect the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated PGGs, but direct quantitative predictions about efficiency are not available.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Wang, T. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games under a risk-transfer mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a risk-transfer (coalition) mechanism into a spatial public goods game can enhance or reduce cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient (r) and the coalition closeness (alpha). In two-strategy systems, coalitional cooperators are more competitive than traditional cooperators, and coalitional defectors are less competitive than traditional defectors. In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Optional participation only provides a narrow scope for sustaining cooperation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, cooperation can only be sustained (and thus group efficiency improved) when nonparticipants have positive outside payoffs and do not harm the public good. The classic 'loner' model is a special case of this. If nonparticipants harm the public good or have negative outside payoffs, cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings highlight the limitations of bottom-up, voluntary participation schemes for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency and cooperation levels in optional participation (loner) variants of the public goods game, which are sometimes considered as alternatives to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control conditions where only optional participation is available, but not the effect of enabling punishment. It also clarifies that optional participation alone only supports cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) under narrow conditions.

- source: Dong, Y. T., He, Z. X., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Constructive agents nullify the ability of destructive agents to foster cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, destructive agents (who harm others at a cost to themselves) can induce cyclic dominance among strategies (cooperation, defection, destruction), which can sustain cooperation and thus increase group efficiency relative to the baseline. However, when constructive agents (who benefit others at a cost to themselves) are introduced, they do not by themselves promote cooperation or efficiency. If both types are present, constructive agents can nullify the ability of destructive agents to sustain cooperation if their benefit exceeds the harm from destructive agents. The efficiency outcome thus depends on the relative magnitude of constructive and destructive effects. The paper provides explicit replicator dynamics and invasion analyses to support these findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with additional positive or negative externalities (constructive or destructive agents), but only indirectly relevant for classic punishment-based games. The 'destructive agent' is adjacent to punishment: it is a non-participant who harms others, but not a targeted, strategic punishment mechanism. The efficiency effects of destructive agents are positive (via cyclic dominance) when their harm is moderate, but are nullified if constructive agents' benefits are larger. The findings suggest that the presence and magnitude of positive and negative externalities can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, because the model is theoretical and does not include classic peer punishment (where players pay to reduce the payoff of specific others in response to their actions), direct quantitative predictions for standard punishment treatments should be made with caution. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented as a non-strategic, non-targeted externality.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of spatial public goods games with insurance mechanisms. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that insurance mechanisms in spatial PGGs can both promote and inhibit cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient, insurance premium, coverage, and the structure of the population. At low synergy coefficients, insurance (especially when purchased by defectors) can sustain cooperation through self-organized cyclic states, increasing group efficiency relative to baseline defection. However, at high synergy coefficients or when insured cooperators are prevalent, insurance can hinder cooperation due to indirect territorial competition. When insurers are allowed to adapt their strategies (two-layer network), the cooperation-promoting effect of insurance is often reduced, as insurers may refuse to transact with defectors, limiting the spread of insurance and altering the evolutionary dynamics. The model provides detailed phase diagrams mapping these effects, with efficiency interpreted as the prevalence of cooperative strategies and the resulting group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how insurance-like risk management mechanisms (adjacent to punishment) affect efficiency in spatial PGGs. While not a direct study of punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games with sanctioning or risk-mitigation features. The results suggest that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to game parameters (e.g., synergy coefficient, insurance cost/benefit, network structure) and the ability of third parties (insurers) to adapt. For prediction tasks, the paper highlights the importance of considering not just the presence of a sanctioning or insurance mechanism, but also who can access it, its cost-effectiveness, and the possibility of coevolution with external agents. The evidence is most directly applicable to spatial, structured populations with risk-mitigation options, and less so to standard punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Wei, M., Wang, X., Liu, L. Z., Zheng, H. W., Jiang, Y. S., Hao, Y. J., Zheng, Z. M., Fu, F., & Tang, S. T. (2025). Indirect reciprocity in the public goods game with collective reputations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with collective reputation and indirect reciprocity, all of the leading-eight social norm strategies can sustain cooperation in social dilemmas when the group assessment criterion (social strictness) is set at a moderate level. Too relaxed or too strict assessment criteria undermine cooperation. The evolutionary success of different strategies and assessment criteria depends on the payoff environment: stricter criteria are favored in low-payoff (high-dilemma) environments, while relaxed criteria are favored as payoffs increase. The model shows that collective reputation can mitigate social dilemmas and that the interplay between game structure and reputation structure is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task by showing that, in public goods games with indirect reciprocity and collective reputation (but without explicit punishment), cooperation and expected payoffs are maximized at moderate levels of social strictness. The findings suggest that the structure of reputation assessment (a design dimension not in the standard list) can play a similar role to punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the absence of explicit punishment means the results cannot be directly transferred to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding how non-punitive, reputation-based mechanisms affect cooperation and payoffs, and for contextualizing the importance of social norm design. It does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2025). Empirical evidence for the spread of Cooperation through copying successful groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that providing information about a successful, cooperative out-group increases participants' contributions in a repeated PGG, but this effect is transient and does not persist or spread within the group. The absence of mechanisms such as punishment means that cooperation is not stably maintained. The authors argue that additional mechanisms (e.g., punishment, reputation) are likely necessary for the stable diffusion and maintenance of cooperation. The cross-cultural survey finds that greater press freedom is associated with higher impersonal prosociality (e.g., general trust), but not with personal prosociality (e.g., family trust). No direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment or on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as punishment is not present in the experimental design. However, it provides indirect support for the idea that, in the absence of punishment, even interventions that increase cooperation (such as exposure to successful out-groups) do not lead to sustained or spreading cooperation or higher group payoffs. The discussion and literature review suggest that punishment may be necessary for stable, high-efficiency outcomes in repeated PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, without punishment, efficiency gains from social learning or imitation are likely to be transient. The experimental design and results can help calibrate the expected baseline (control) efficiency in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the treatment effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Grayson, S., Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Zaki, J. (2025). Ironic effects of prosocial gossip in driving inaccurate social perceptions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game, participants overwhelmingly use gossip to highlight free-riders rather than cooperators, even when most group members are cooperative. When a new set of participants reads these gossip notes, they systematically underestimate the cooperativeness of the original group, becoming more cynical and less accurate in their perceptions. However, this increased cynicism does not translate into lower expectations for their own group's cooperation or reduced contributions in their own public goods game. There is some evidence that exposure to negative gossip polarizes behavior, increasing the likelihood of both full cooperation and full defection, but the average contribution is not significantly affected.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or gossip on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it provides evidence that gossip (as a reputational mechanism) is negatively skewed and can distort outsiders' perceptions of group cooperativeness, but does not affect actual contribution behavior in subsequent games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that reputational mechanisms like gossip may increase cooperation (as shown in the referenced Feinberg et al., 2014 study), but the current paper's main contribution is to highlight the informational and perceptual side effects of such mechanisms, not their impact on efficiency.

- source: Coutts, A. (2022). Identifying communication spillovers in lab-in-the-field experiments&x2729;. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that communication spillovers between villages (i.e., when participants in one village can talk to those in a neighboring village who have already played) lead to substantial increases in contributions to the public good. The effect is robust to different matching strategies and distance thresholds. In the second round, when some villages played alternative versions of the public goods game (including punishment, reward, or risk), the positive effect of these treatments on contributions was only observed in villages not exposed to communication spillovers. The main implication is that communication between sessions can significantly increase cooperation, potentially biasing estimates of treatment effects in field experiments. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication (even unstructured, informal communication between sessions) can increase cooperation in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that allowing or enabling communication (or failing to prevent spillovers) may increase contributions, which could translate into higher efficiency if all other game parameters are held constant. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, and only analyzes contribution rates, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a main treatment, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of controlling for communication and spillovers when interpreting the effects of any treatment (including punishment) in field experiments.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2024). Evolutionary Dynamics of Preguidance Strategies in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper introduces and analyzes two forms of preemptive guidance strategies (peer and pool guidance) in a one-shot public goods game with a pregame negotiation phase. Both forms of guidance can reduce the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders (cooperators who do not guide). Pool guidance is more effective than peer guidance, especially at higher guidance costs. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on group payoff or efficiency as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that preemptive guidance strategies can reduce free riding and increase the prevalence of guidance strategies in PGGs, especially when guidance costs are low. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The guidance strategies studied are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the dynamics of pre-commitment or preemptive intervention, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes from punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Battu, B. (2023). Co-evolution of conditional cooperation and social norm. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model finds that high initial cooperation and low contribution costs favor the emergence and stability of high cooperation and moderate norm sensitivity. When contribution costs are high or initial cooperation is low, cooperation fails to establish. The noise parameter (imperfect information about past cooperation) can modulate but not destroy cooperation unless it is very high. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, norm enforcement is internalized as psychic costs for free-riding, which depend on the number of cooperators, the cost of contribution, and individual norm sensitivity. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how internalized norm enforcement (psychic costs) and conditional cooperation can sustain cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially under low contribution costs and high initial cooperation. However, it does not model or report the effects of explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of initial conditions, contribution cost, and norm sensitivity for the emergence of cooperation, but not for quantifying efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hu, D., Liu, J. Z., Liu, C., & Chu, C. (2025). Dynamic incentives and environmental feedback in public goods games: Promoting cooperation through critical thresholds. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing dynamic, group-level environmental feedback (bonuses or penalties based on collective contribution thresholds) in a spatial public goods game strongly promotes cooperation, especially when the synergy factor is low. The effect is not due to individual punishment or reward, but to a group-level adjustment of payoffs. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies and incentives. The model shows that higher expectation thresholds for group contributions can, counterintuitively, make cooperation more likely, and that the system transitions from defection to cooperation as the dynamic incentive increases. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. Instead, it models a group-level dynamic incentive (environmental feedback) that is not targeted at individuals and is not costly to assign. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how group-level feedback mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The results are most relevant for adjacent mechanisms (dynamic group-level incentives) rather than direct punishment or sanctions.

- source: Sun, X. P., Zhu, H. R., Kang, H. W., Bi, Y. Z., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of memory reputation-induced tax and reward allocation on spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-driven mechanism for adjusting local synergy factors in a spatial public goods game can significantly promote cooperation. The effect is strongest when the maximum possible synergy factor is high, the minimum is low, the adjustment step size is small, and the reputation threshold is moderate. Both excessively high and low reputation thresholds hinder cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, reputation indirectly affects payoffs by modulating the local multiplier. The main reported outcome is the proportion of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. However, it is relevant for understanding how endogenous, reputation-driven changes to the payoff structure (via the synergy factor) can affect cooperation rates. The findings suggest that indirect, reputation-based incentives can substitute for or complement explicit punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation, but the results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only adjacent, indirect evidence.

- source: Samu, F., Piras, S., Righi, S., Setti, M., & Takács, K. (2025). Cooperation is not rewarded by friendship, but generous and selfish students repel each other in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a sample of 10-year-old students, measured cooperation and prosociality in economic games (including a PGG) do not predict receiving more friendship nominations or being avoided via negative ties. There is no evidence that prosocial students are more popular or that selfish students are more disliked. However, there is a significant tendency for students with dissimilar levels of generosity (especially in the Dictator Game) to form negative ties with each other, suggesting that social network repulsion is structured by differences in prosociality. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it uses a standard PGG to measure individual cooperation, the outcomes are social network nominations (friendship and negative ties), not payoffs or efficiency. The only adjacent relevance is the interpretation of negative ties as a form of informal social punishment, but this is not a formal or incentivized punishment mechanism as in PGG experiments with punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). A social monitoring mechanism for third-party judges promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward, cooperation and group payoff decline over time, consistent with previous literature. The authors reconstruct a micro-dynamic model based on proportional changes in expected payoff, showing that players' willingness to cooperate depends on their current strategy and local cooperation levels. The model predicts the observed decline in cooperation and group payoff. The paper also documents a 'restart effect' in two-stage games, where cooperation temporarily increases at the transition point. The findings provide a framework for predicting the evolution of cooperation and group payoff in repeated PGGs without external interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on baseline efficiency and group payoff dynamics in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly relevant for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related variables. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it is highly informative for modeling the baseline (control) case.

- source: Koessler, A. K., Müller, J., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2023). Asymmetric heterogeneities and the role of transfers in a public goods experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with three players (one high-endowment/low-productivity, two low-endowment/high-productivity), enabling voluntary, unconditional transfers before contribution decisions leads to higher group efficiency and average payoffs compared to a baseline with no transfers and a condition with exogenous (non-voluntary) transfers. Most high-endowment players transfer all or half of their endowment, and recipients reciprocate with high contributions, resulting in larger public good provision and higher group profits. The effect is not observed when transfers are exogenously imposed, highlighting the importance of intention-based reciprocity. If the transfer option is available but not used, cooperation and efficiency are lower than in the baseline. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctions, introducing a voluntary transfer mechanism in a PGG with asymmetric heterogeneity can substantially increase efficiency and group payoffs, provided that transfers are used and reciprocated. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar heterogeneity and transfer options, but do not inform about the effects of punishment or sanctions, as these are not present in the design. The results suggest that mechanisms enabling trust and reciprocity (such as voluntary transfers) can substitute for punishment in boosting efficiency, but the effect is contingent on the use and perception of the transfer. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for understanding the efficiency effects of alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms in heterogeneous PGGs.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Wang, J. W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., Chen, W., Xu, W. S., Dai, W. H., & Ming, Y. X. (2023). Promotion, Disintegration and Remediation of group cooperation under heterogeneous distribution system based on peer rating. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Redistribution rules that reward willingness to cooperate (high alpha) and are neutral with respect to initial endowment (beta near zero) lead to the highest group efficiency (average payoff) and lowest inequality. Redistribution that favors the poor (negative beta) or the rich (positive beta) increases inequality and does not maximize group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning; all effects are due to redistribution of payoffs after the public goods game. The findings challenge the assumption that subsidizing the poor in such games always promotes cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it is highly informative about how redistribution mechanisms (distinct from punishment) affect efficiency and cooperation in heterogeneous populations. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it does provide a useful contrast: redistribution based on willingness to cooperate can increase efficiency, while redistribution based on endowment does not. The game design dimensions are well specified for redistribution, but not for punishment.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., Bender, C., Langenbucher, K., Vollmeyer, R., & Schultze, M. (2024). Facets of altruism in study majors and volunteering groups. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that allowing heterogeneous investment and dynamic feedback on the multiplication factor in spatial public goods games can substantially increase both cooperation and group-level success (measured by average payoff and group success). High feedback strength and moderate group-preference/half-success effort are optimal for group success and individual welfare. The model recovers the standard spatial PGG when feedback is disabled. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are included or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for understanding how dynamic feedback and heterogeneous investment affect efficiency and payoff in public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. Its findings may be useful for predicting efficiency in PGGs with dynamic returns, but not for the specific prediction task of punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency. It provides no evidence on punishment, so should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

  findings: The study finds that in public goods games simulated with deep Q-learning agents, the rate of cooperation increases as group size increases, and also as MPCR increases. However, the cooperation rate plateaus at around 50% for large group sizes or high MPCR. The model does not include punishment, reward, or communication mechanisms. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are consistent with some human experiments, but the model is limited to binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices and does not address payoff-based efficiency directly.
