It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Gou, Z. Q., & Li, Y. (2023). Prisoner's dilemma game model Based on historical strategy information. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that increasing the length of historical memory (M) and the weight of memory information (β) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to higher cooperation rates and more stable cooperative clusters. The effect saturates at high memory lengths, and excessive memory does not further promote cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rate and strategy stability, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. - source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.

  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.

The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. - source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.

  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.

Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yan, M. H., Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2023). Doing what others do does not stabilize continuous norms. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in models where norm-relevant behaviors are continuous (e.g., contribution levels in a public goods context), similarity-based payoffs (including social sanctions or punishment for deviating from the mean) do not stabilize arbitrary or costly norms. Instead, the equilibrium norm is determined by direct effects (such as individual or group payoffs, moral preferences, or cognitive/cultural attractors). Only in discrete norm spaces, or when categorization or multiple cognitive attractors are present, can similarity-based payoffs maintain multiple stable equilibria or persistent between-group differences. For public goods-like environments, this suggests that punishment or social sanctions alone are insufficient to maintain high-cooperation norms unless additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, discrete categories, or multiple optima) are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in continuous public goods games or similar environments, enabling punishment or social sanctions (modeled as similarity-based payoffs) will not, by itself, maintain high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibria. The equilibrium will be determined by direct payoff incentives, not by the presence of punishment, unless the game is discretized or there are additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, cognitive attractors) that create multiple optima. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in continuous-choice PGGs, the presence of punishment may not increase efficiency unless the design includes discrete choices, strong moral framing, or other mechanisms that create stable high-cooperation equilibria. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, but it strongly suggests that punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains in continuous settings.

  findings: The paper finds that large-scale cooperation (hundreds of individuals) in the production of public goods (e.g., communal hunts, construction of drivelines, warfare, habitat improvement) was common in Holocene and likely Pleistocene foraging societies, even at low population densities. Mechanisms such as direct sanctions (ostracism, denial of aid, physical punishment), social norms, and leadership roles were important for sustaining cooperation beyond the scale where mutualistic or reciprocal incentives suffice. The evidence challenges the view that ancestral human cooperation was limited to small groups. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that would directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.

However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, MPCR, player count) on efficiency. Thus, while it supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups, it does not provide data that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like experiments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of sanctions and institutions for large-scale cooperation and the likely need for additional mechanisms as group size increases.

  findings: The review finds that both social and nonsocial uncertainty can reduce prosocial behaviors by providing excuses for selfishness or increasing loss aversion, but in some contexts, uncertainty can also increase prosociality (e.g., by promoting forgiveness or overestimating one's impact on others). Coping strategies for social uncertainty include impression formation, impression updating, and compliance with social norms. Punishment and reward are discussed as norm enforcement tools, but the review does not analyze their effects on efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding mechanisms and generating hypotheses about the role of uncertainty and norm enforcement.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that reputation systems can incentivize cooperation through two distinct mechanisms: 'pull' (where evaluators are motivated to cooperate with good actors for direct reciprocal benefit) and 'push' (where evaluators are motivated by normative pressure to cooperate with good actors to maintain their own reputation). It reviews how these mechanisms map onto different game-theoretic models (e.g., coordination games, indirect reciprocity) and discusses their implications for psychology, behavior, and the design of reputation-based interventions. The framework is used to interpret existing empirical findings and to generate predictions about when and how reputation will promote cooperation or punishment, but does not test these predictions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers a conceptual framework that may help interpret when and why reputation, cooperation, and punishment mechanisms (including those in PGGs) are likely to be effective, depending on whether incentives are primarily 'pull' or 'push'. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the structure of the game (e.g., whether it is a coordination game, whether social norms are salient, whether actions are observable) will moderate the effectiveness of reputation and punishment, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

The presence of exemplary cooperators increases the resilience of cooperation and can enhance the 'quality' of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how non-punitive, positive influence strategies affect cooperation, but are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. - source: Yang, Z. H., & Zhang, L. P. (2023). Random migration with tie retention promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or model punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that network structure, migration, and tie retention can strongly affect cooperation rates in the prisoner's dilemma game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

The model is applied to obligate and facultative mutualisms, arguing that metacommunity structure and weight-of-numbers dispersal can allow mutualists to outcompete cheaters at the community level. Thus, its value for the prediction task is mainly conceptual and mechanistic, not quantitative.

- source: Li, O., Shi, Y., & Li, K. (2025). Red, rather than blue can promote fairness in decision-making. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the color red (vs. blue) increases (vs. decreases) the amount offered by proposers in the ultimatum game, and this effect is mediated by perceived competitiveness. In the impunity game, where responders cannot punish proposers by rejecting offers, color has no effect on offers. The results suggest that contextual cues like color can influence fair behavior in allocation games, but only when strategic motives (i.e., the threat of rejection/punishment) are present. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not use a public goods game or direct punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers indirect evidence that contextual cues (like color) can affect proposer behavior in allocation games when strategic punishment is possible (as in the UG), but not when it is absent (as in the IG). The findings suggest that the presence or absence of punishment mechanisms (or their analogs) can moderate the effect of contextual factors on fair behavior, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that receivers' interpretation of gossip motives (pro-social vs. pro-self) significantly shapes their trust in both the sender and the target of gossip. Correctly interpreting pro-social motives leads to higher trust in the sender, while pro-self motives reduce trust. The ability to overcome intuition (measured by CRT) increases the likelihood of correctly identifying pro-social motives. However, the study does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The behavioral outcome is the amount invested in a trust game vignette, which is not linked to real payoffs or group efficiency.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

These differences affect the stability and flexibility of norms, the ease of norm change, and the likelihood of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical data or quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but may inform hypotheses about when punishment is likely to be more or less effective based on the type of norm and cognitive demands.

- source: Bae, M., Shimada, T., & Baek, S. K. (2024). Exact cluster dynamics of indirect reciprocity in complete graphs. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper provides an exact theoretical analysis of how different indirect reciprocity norms (L4 and L6) shape the long-run social structure in large, fully connected populations. Under L6 (stern judging), the population almost always segregates into two mutually hostile clusters as the group size increases, due to the entropy of balanced configurations. Under L4, a subtle change in the assessment rule leads to a high probability of the 'paradise' state, where everyone assesses everyone else positively, especially as group size increases. The findings are about the stationary distribution of assessment configurations and the emergence of social structure, not about efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it is highly informative about the structural consequences of different indirect reciprocity norms (i.e., whether societies segregate or reach universal cooperation), it does not model or report payoffs, efficiency, or welfare. The results may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which cooperation is structurally stable, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that mutualism is a reliable, stable, and discriminant psychological construct, with four components: partner choice, proportional sharing, proportional punishment, and proportional helping. Higher mutualism scores predict attitudinal opposition to equal sharing and redistribution, and preference for proportional division in hypothetical scenarios. The mutualism scale is largely independent of compassion, envy, self-interest, and utilitarianism. The findings are robust across two large samples. However, all outcomes are attitudinal or psychometric; there is no evidence on actual behavior in public goods games, punishment games, or on efficiency/welfare outcomes.

- source: Benard, S., Doan, L., Nicholson, D. A., Meanwell, E., Wright, E. L., & Lista, P. (2024). An Eye For an Eye Versus Turning The Other Cheek? The Status Consequences of Revenge and Forgiveness in Intergroup Conflict. *Social Forces*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Forgiveness in intergroup conflict is perceived as more status-worthy than revenge, across national, political, and sports identities. (2) Justifying actions as group-motivated (versus individually motivated) has little effect on perceived status, except for a small positive effect for forgiveness in the sports scenario. (3) Open-ended responses show that forgiving actors are more often described as competent, warm, and high-status, while vengeful actors are more often described as dominant and conflict-seeking. (4) There is some evidence that group-motivated revenge is seen as making the group look bad. (5) The study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any material or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it conceptually links intergroup conflict to public goods problems and discusses status as a selective incentive, all outcomes are attitudinal (perceived status, trait attributions) rather than behavioral or payoff-based. There is no manipulation of actual contribution, punishment, or reward in a game-theoretic or economic sense, and no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Thus, the paper is not informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games with or without punishment.

  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral motivation evolved in two forms: partial (toward close others) and impartial (toward the group or community at large). It proposes that social norms and sanctions (including third-party punishment and internalized sanctions such as guilt and shame) emerged at the community level and were later internalized, giving rise to the feeling of moral obligation. The authors critique and build on the accounts of Tomasello and Darwall, emphasizing the group-level origins of normativity and the psychological mechanisms that support norm compliance. The paper does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it present experimental or empirical evidence.

Its relevance is limited to theoretical insights about the evolutionary function of sanctions and norm internalization in supporting norm compliance and cooperation. However, it may provide useful conceptual background on why sanctions (including internalized ones) can support norm compliance.

- source: Lee, H. W., & Weng, Y. N. (2025). Granular Q-learning adaptation boosts collective welfare in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that more granular, neighbor-specific Q-learning (ID) enables agents to achieve higher collective welfare (average payoff) and maintain cooperation more robustly than aggregated Q-learning (II) in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma games. This advantage is most pronounced at intermediate dilemma strengths and in homogeneous networks, but persists in scale-free networks as well. The results are based on extensive simulations varying network structure, dilemma strength, and agent composition. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present; the only difference is the learning architecture. The findings suggest that the granularity of learning adaptation is a key moderator of efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial and scale-free Prisoner's Dilemma environments, the granularity of learning adaptation (neighbor-specific vs. aggregated) is a major determinant of collective efficiency. While not a public goods game or a punishment study, the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the learning rule or information granularity. The absence of punishment/reward means the findings are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome and the focus on learning architecture offer useful indirect evidence for how cognitive sophistication or information structure can boost efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings where agent adaptation, rather than explicit sanctions, is the main intervention.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

Experimental inductions of state compassion or pride do not reliably shift these social preferences. - source: Yang, X. H., Huang, H. Y., Zhang, Y. C., Wang, J. S., Guan, J. H., & Zhou, S. G. (2023). Short Memory-Based Human Strategy Modeling in Social Dilemmas. *Mathematics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that individual accumulated payoff is the most important predictor of cooperation/defection decisions, more so than the outcome of the previous round. Behavioral clustering improves the predictability of individual moves, with F1-scores for predicting cooperation/defection reaching up to 95% in some clusters. The diversity of human strategies is substantial, but clustering allows for more accurate modeling. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding individual decision-making and the role of payoff history, rather than for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with optional partner selection (refusal to play), which is only weakly analogous to punishment. The main insight is that accumulated payoff is a strong driver of individual cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding the importance of payoff history in individual decision-making, but not for the core prediction task.

  findings: The experiment finds that both communication and subsidies for NCH can shift groups toward more cooperative, higher-efficiency outcomes, but also that some groups coordinate on Nash equilibria (heavy spraying) that are also efficient in the game’s payoff structure. Group-level characteristics are more predictive of cooperative behavior than individual traits. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks involving communication or positive incentives, the findings are more relevant.

- source: Robbins, B., Karell, D., Siegenthaler, S., & Kamm, A. (2023). Pathways to prosocial leadership: an online experiment on the effects of external subsidies and the relative price of giving. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: External subsidies (private piece-rate transfers) to leaders increase the proportion of community tokens given to recipients, thus increasing group welfare. The effect is robust to the timing of subsidy introduction or removal, with no evidence of crowding out or reduced giving after subsidy removal. The relative price of giving (multiplier) does not significantly affect giving. Norms about giving are not affected by either subsidies or the multiplier. The design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game, but the main outcome is directly related to group welfare/efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that external subsidies to leaders (akin to positive incentives or rewards) can increase group welfare in resource allocation settings, even when leaders have full discretion. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves redistribution rather than voluntary contribution to a public pool. The findings suggest that positive incentives can increase efficiency, but cannot be directly transferred to punishment effects. The absence of punishment, the dictator game structure, and the focus on leader-driven redistribution limit its direct applicability to PGG-like punishment predictions, but the evidence is informative for the effect of positive incentives on efficiency.

  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.

Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. - source: Lie-Panis, J., & André, J. B. (2022). Cooperation as a signal of time preferences. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which cooperation is a signal of time preferences in a repeated trust game. Individuals with lower discount rates (more future-oriented) are more likely to cooperate, and populations with lower average discount rates can sustain cooperation in a wider range of parameter settings (e.g., lower cost-benefit ratios, less reliable observation). The model provides payoff-based conditions for the evolutionary stability of cooperation and explains variability in cooperation and trust as a function of time preferences. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is modeled. The main outcome is the equilibrium pay-off for different strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or a public goods game. However, it is relevant for understanding how individual and population-level time preferences can affect the stability and variability of cooperation and aggregate pay-offs in repeated trust-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment or public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that time preferences and the structure of observation and reputation can moderate cooperation and efficiency, but it does not address punishment or sanctions.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Gerson, J., Powdthavee, N., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2023). Large losses from little lies: Strategic gender misrepresentation and cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are randomly allowed to misrepresent their gender to their partner in a one-shot Golden Balls game, the average defection (steal) rate increases by about 10-12 percentage points compared to control conditions (blind or true gender revealed). Those who actually choose to misrepresent are about 32 percentage points more likely to defect. The possibility of misrepresentation also increases defection among those who do not misrepresent, due to increased distrust. The result is a substantial reduction in overall cooperation and group welfare, as measured by the proportion of participants receiving the maximum possible payoff (£10 each for mutual cooperation) and the average payment per participant. The effect is robust across lab and online samples, and is not explained by differences in personality traits or risk/trust attitudes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing opportunities for strategic misrepresentation of identity (here, gender) in a one-shot social dilemma with communication reduces cooperation and group welfare, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. While not a public goods game, the Golden Balls game is a close variant of the prisoner's dilemma, and the findings are likely to generalize to other one-shot or anonymous social dilemmas with communication. The results suggest that any design feature that increases uncertainty about partner identity or intentions (such as allowing misrepresentation) can reduce efficiency. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling deception or increasing uncertainty will lower efficiency, even if punishment is not present. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is included in the design.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Jimenez-Romero, C., & Page, K. M. (2022). Dying to cooperate: the role of environmental harshness in human collaboration. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that environmental harshness is a key moderator of cooperative behavior in coordination games like the Stag Hunt. In both models and human experiments, individuals are more likely to choose cooperative, high-risk strategies when the environment is harsh and survival is threatened. Communication between agents (or players) allows for better coordination and increases the viability of cooperation. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms, and does not use a public goods game structure, but rather a coordination game with binary choices and pairwise matching. The main outcomes are behavioral (strategy choice and switching), with survival probability and energy reserves as related but not directly group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative context about how environmental harshness and communication affect the viability of cooperation in coordination games. Since punishment and reward are not present, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the role of environmental context and communication in cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Schippers, A. L., & Soetevent, A. R. (2024). Sharing with minimal regulation? Evidence from neighborhood book exchange. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: Neighborhood book exchanges operate with minimal regulation and no formal punishment or sanctioning. Despite this, users maintain high levels of reciprocal exchange, returning nearly as many books as they take, and of similar value. Free riding exists but is limited. Owners' restocking compensates for the small deficit. Social norms and strong cooperative preferences among participants are likely responsible for this outcome. The system is stable over time, and a supply shock of high-quality books does not lead to persistent changes in usage or quality. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can be sustained in some real-world sharing environments, likely due to strong norms and self-selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a real-world, unregulated sharing environment with no punishment or sanctioning, efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can remain high if strong social norms and cooperative preferences are present. However, the setting is only adjacent to public goods games: there is no explicit group payoff, no monetary contributions, and no formal punishment. The findings suggest that the absence of punishment does not necessarily lead to collapse in efficiency, but this may depend on context-specific factors like self-selection and social norms. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that in environments lacking punishment, efficiency may be higher than standard PGG models predict if strong informal norms exist, but the transferability to standard PGGs is limited.

- source: Lu, W., & Liang, S. (2025). The role of strategy-affected emotions in the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial three-strategy prisoner's dilemma model with emotion dynamics, increasing the intensity of friendliness (the degree to which strategies affect emotions) leads to higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs in the population. The effect is robust across different parameterizations of the game (including donor-recipient and boundary PD variants). Memory length moderates the effect: with low friendliness, longer memory increases cooperation; with high friendliness, longer memory decreases cooperation. The model demonstrates that stable, high-payoff cooperation can emerge through emotion-strategy feedbacks alone, without any punishment or reward mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is relevant as a theoretical demonstration that high cooperation and group payoff can be achieved through emotion-based mechanisms, without punishment or reward. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper suggests that alternative mechanisms (like friendliness) can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency, but it does not inform how punishment would change efficiency relative to a control. The game design dimensions relevant to punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech, etc.) are not present or manipulated. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment or reward.

- source: Chen, W. M., Quan, J., Wang, X. J., & Liu, Y. (2023). Evolutionary dynamics from fluctuating environments with deterministic and stochastic noises. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of evolutionary dynamics in the hawk-dove game under fluctuating environments with both payoff and demographic noise. Through simulation, it finds that both deterministic and stochastic fluctuations can promote cooperation, sometimes even reversing selection so that cooperation dominates. Deterministic fluctuations are more effective than stochastic ones at promoting cooperation. The mechanism is that noise can create unfair time-averaged payoffs that favor cooperation. High variance in noise promotes cooperation but makes its maintenance less stable. The results are robust to the type of noise distribution. The paper briefly extends the analysis to the prisoner's dilemma, finding similar qualitative results. No public goods game, punishment, or explicit group efficiency analysis is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, punishment, or group efficiency. Its findings about noise and cooperation in the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how environmental uncertainty affects cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Lu, Y. K., Hong, L. J., & Shi, L. (2025). Evolution of cooperation with asymmetric rewards. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asymmetric rewards in a two-population prisoner's dilemma model can sustain cooperation and prevent total defection, especially in structured populations (lattices). The effect of asymmetry on cooperation depends on the dilemma strength: under strong dilemma (high temptation to defect), asymmetry promotes cooperation; under weak dilemma, strong asymmetry can actually inhibit cooperation. The model includes both prosocial and antisocial rewards but does not include punishment or sanctions. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study punishment, public goods games, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on asymmetric rewards in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) can sustain cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency. The game structure, outcome measures, and intervention type (reward, not punishment) are all different from the target prediction context.

  findings: The study finds that certain conditional cooperation strategies, especially those tolerant of cooperative behavior and harsh toward defection (the 'catalyst strategy'), can promote high levels of cooperation in indirect reciprocity models based on second-order action labels. The presence of noise (action or label errors) reduces cooperation rates but does not qualitatively change the system's state. Larger populations enhance the effectiveness of the catalyst strategy, while smaller populations and higher noise diminish it. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and efficiency or group payoff is not a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings on cooperation rates in indirect reciprocity models with second-order action labels may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how certain strategy structures (e.g., tolerance and harshness) affect cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The absence of payoff/efficiency as a central outcome and the lack of a punishment mechanism mean this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a community learning mechanism—where individuals preferentially imitate the most successful community members—substantially increases the proportion of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulation. The effect is strongest when the minimum community size is small and when individuals are more likely to join large, distant communities (low distance cost). The emergence of large, cohesive communities is associated with high cooperation rates. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the model.

The weighting parameter ω, which balances historical best and neighbor imitation, has a non-monotonic effect: low ω promotes cooperation when temptation to defect is low, while high ω helps sustain cooperation when temptation is high. The results are robust across weak and true prisoner's dilemma parameterizations. However, the paper does not study punishment, reward, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.

It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies cooperation dynamics in a related social dilemma (prisoner's dilemma) on networks, with a focus on strategy update rules and the divisibility of cooperation (continuous vs. all-or-nothing). The findings may inform expectations about the impact of strategy space (continuous vs. binary) on cooperation, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively switch between two payoff matrices with different dilemma intensities, based on their aspiration level, can significantly promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. There exists an optimal range of aspiration levels that maximizes cooperation. Too low or too high aspiration levels lead to lower cooperation, as defectors dominate. The mechanism is robust to changes in the temptation parameter and network size. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings about adaptive switching between dilemma intensities and the role of aspiration may be conceptually relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in perceived dilemma strength or adaptive game structure can affect cooperation rates, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and results are adjacent to, but not directly transferable to, the target prediction task.

  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to adapt the intensity of their interactions based on satisfaction with payoffs from neighbors can greatly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma. There are optimal values of the satisfaction parameter (\alpha) that maximize cooperation, and both too low and too high values of \alpha reduce cooperation. The mechanism works by creating positive feedback between satisfaction and interaction among cooperators, leading to robust cooperative clusters. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments.   findings: The paper finds that requiring additional cash payments from homeowners (when the reserve fund is insufficient) increases the consent rate for maintenance projects. This effect is robust across OLS, IV, and Tobit models, and is stronger for projects proposed by homeowners' associations and for elevator maintenance. The mechanism appears to be increased salience and information provision, which motivates participation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment/sanctions, and the outcome is the behavioral approval rate, not a payoff-based measure.

The findings are about how additional required payments (a form of cost salience or information) increase behavioral consent rates in a real-world collective action setting. While the context is adjacent to public goods games, the lack of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may be useful for understanding how cost salience or information affects participation, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Nie, J. C., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2022). Keeping or reversing social norms promote cooperation by enhancing indirect reciprocity. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: Children aged 6-9 are more likely to cooperate with in-group members than with out-group members in an iterated face-to-face Prisoner's Dilemma Game. Girls generally cooperate more than boys, and the sensitivity to group membership varies by age and gender. Cooperation is also more likely following a partner's cooperative choice. The study does not include punishment, reward, or analyze efficiency or group payoff; all outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/defection).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how group identity, gender, and age affect cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation rates in iterated dyadic games among children, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

  findings: The paper finds that, in spatial prisoner's dilemma games with Q-learning, mixed strategy games consistently support higher levels of cooperation than continuous strategy games, and both outperform discrete strategy games as the temptation to defect increases. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or any explicit sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The study finds that both higher interaction diversity (more link-type players) and more frequent information updating in the information layer can increase the fraction of cooperators in the population. The effect of information updating is most pronounced when interaction diversity is low.   findings: The study finds that gender has little impact on the development of prosociality and fairness in children aged 4-15 across a wide range of societies. In both the Dictator Game (measuring prosocial choice) and the Inequity Games (measuring aversion to advantageous and disadvantageous inequity), males and females behave similarly, with only small and unreliable differences observed. There is also little evidence for substantial societal variation in gender differences. The findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (choices, rejections) and do not address efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental designs.

Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or similar environments.

- source: Jones, M. I., & Christakis, N. A. (2024). Bringing leaders of network subgroups closer together does not facilitate consensus. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing noise in the information network makes it less likely for groups to reach consensus and increases the time required to do so. The geodesic distance between leaders of opposing subgroups does not have a significant effect on the probability or speed of consensus formation. Leadership labeling affects individual behavior, making leaders more likely to compromise when their neighbors do, but this does not translate into group-level differences in consensus based on leader distance. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency/payoff-based outcome. The findings are specific to consensus formation in a networked coordination game with leader labeling and noise, and do not transfer to the prediction of efficiency under punishment in PGG or similar games.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., & Schulhofer-Wohl, J. (2022). The impact of distance on parochial altruism: An experimental investigation. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that parochial altruism (allocation to the between-group account, which benefits the in-group and harms the out-group) is higher when both in-group and out-group distances are high. Low distance to the out-group reduces both altruism and parochial altruism. The main behavioral outcomes are the propensity and amount allocated to within-group and between-group accounts. There is no analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are robust to alternative distance measures and controls for group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The behavioral outcomes (parochial altruism, altruism) are not directly transferable to payoff-based efficiency predictions. The game is an intergroup conflict variant (IPD-MD), not a standard PGG, and the main manipulations concern group distance, not institutional design features like punishment or reward. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Miranda-Rodríguez, R. A., Leenen, I., Han, H., Palafox-Palafox, G., & García-Rodríguez, G. (2023). Moral reasoning and moral competence as predictors of cooperative behavior in a social dilemma. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in an iterated stranger PDG, the probability of cooperation is strongly influenced by the outcome of previous rounds: negative experiences (sucker, punishment, temptation) reduce cooperation, while positive experiences (reward) increase it. However, individuals with higher levels of moral reasoning (DIT-2 N2-index) and moral competence (MCT C-score) are less likely to reduce cooperation after negative experiences, particularly after sucker outcomes. There is no main effect of moral reasoning or competence on overall cooperation rates, but both moderate the effect of negative experiences. The study does not examine efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are limited to individual-level predictors of cooperation in a repeated PDG without punishment or reward. The study is only indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, and does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Candia, C., Oyarzún, M., Landaeta, V., Yaikin, T., Monge, C., Hidalgo, C., & Rodriguez-Sickert, C. (2022). Reciprocity heightens academic performance in elementary school students. *Heliyon*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
