  findings: The study finds that students who engage in more reciprocated cooperative relationships (as measured by a non-anonymous, continuous-token Prisoner's Dilemma game) tend to have higher subsequent academic performance (GPA). This association is robust to controls for prior GPA, attendance, social status, and other confounders, and is confirmed using a difference-in-differences approach. The effect is stronger for students in the top 20% of reciprocated cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based but rather focus on mapping social relationships and predicting academic performance. The game is a non-anonymous, dyadic, continuous-token social dilemma, which is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs or similar games.

- source: Liao, H. M., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., Guo, N., & Ling, X. (2023). Cooperative evolution under the joint influence of local popularity and global popularity. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the weight of local popularity and the scaling index of popularity in the fitness function can significantly affect the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial WPDG. When the scaling index is positive, high weight on local popularity promotes cooperation, while global popularity is detrimental. When the scaling index is negative, global popularity can help small clusters of cooperators survive even in harsh environments. There is no analysis of punishment, reward, or group efficiency/payoff. The results are entirely about the prevalence of cooperation, not about group welfare or surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment, reward, or any efficiency/welfare outcome. Its findings about local/global popularity and cooperation rates in spatial WPDG are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Radkani, S., Tenenbaum, J. B., & Saxe, R. (2025). What people learn from punishment: A cognitive model. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that observers' inferences about the wrongness of an act and the motives of an authority after observing punishment are interdependent and strongly shaped by prior beliefs about norms and authority. Punishment can communicate norm violation and enhance or damage the authority's reputation, depending on context. The authors' Bayesian inverse planning model quantitatively predicts these belief updates and outperforms alternative models. The study also shows that repeated observation of punishment can either reduce or sustain polarization in beliefs, depending on initial priors. However, all outcomes are belief-based; the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about belief updating and interpretation of punishment, not about actual contributions, cooperation, or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how punishment is perceived or how norm communication and legitimacy are inferred, but not for payoff-based outcome prediction.

- source: Brouwer, T., Galeotti, F., & Villeval, M. C. (2023). Teaching Norms: Direct Evidence of Parental Transmission. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: Parents are significantly more likely to directly punish a norm violator (verbal confrontation) and to help a stranger in need when their child is present, compared to when they are alone. There is no significant increase in indirect punishment (withholding help) in the presence of a child. The findings support the idea that parents use opportunities to teach social norms to their children by modeling both norm enforcement and prosocial behavior. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use a PGG or any group payoff structure, nor does it report on efficiency or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to understanding behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement and punishment in naturalistic settings, not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in economic games.

- source: Alvarez-Benjumea, A., & Valentim, V. (2024). The Enforcement of Political Norms. *British Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that individuals in Spain perceive public displays of support for the radical-right party Vox as less socially and morally appropriate and more harmful than support for other parties. Respondents are more likely to view sanctioning (especially indirect sanctions like gossip and avoidance) as appropriate and report greater willingness to engage in such sanctions against radical-right supporters. The willingness to sanction is not strongly predicted by political ideology or interest, but is correlated with age, gender, education, local support for Vox, and emotional reactions (anger, disgust). The study does not measure or discuss any payoff-based or efficiency outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or similar economic game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it offers detailed insights into the social and psychological mechanisms of norm enforcement and sanctioning in a political context, it does not use a game-theoretic or economic game design, nor does it report any group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG or similar environments.

- source: Gächter, S., Molleman, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2025). Why people follow rules. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that 55-70% of participants follow an arbitrary costly rule even when acting anonymously and alone, and when rule-breaking has no consequences for others. Social expectations and intrinsic respect for rules explain most rule-following. Adding externalities (harm to a charity) or punishment (with varying probabilities) increases rule-following rates, but a substantial baseline of rule-following exists without these. Observing peer rule-breaking reduces rule-following, but even with all peers breaking the rule, a significant fraction still comply. The main outcome is behavioral (rule-following), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. While it provides evidence that punishment increases rule-following in a single-player, non-strategic, asocial rule-following task, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the context is not a PGG or its variant. The findings may be relevant for understanding the motivational basis of compliance and the effect of sanctions on rule-following, but cannot be directly transferred to efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Berger, J. (2024). Social capital is associated with cooperation and indirect norm enforcement in the field: behavioural evidence from Switzerland. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that neighborhoods with higher social capital exhibit higher rates of cooperation (as measured by the lost-letter technique) and more frequent indirect norm enforcement (as measured by bystanders picking up litter after a staged transgression). Direct enforcement (verbal confrontation) is rare and does not differ significantly between neighborhoods. Residents in high social capital neighborhoods have higher empirical and normative expectations of norm compliance and lower fear of retaliation. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any payoff-based group interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about real-world cooperation and informal norm enforcement, not about payoff, efficiency, or sanctioning in economic games. The norm enforcement studied is indirect (e.g., picking up litter) and not costly punishment as in PGGs. Therefore, while the paper may be contextually relevant for understanding the social foundations of cooperation and informal enforcement, it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Schläpfer, A. (2025). The social benefits of anti-social punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study documents that cultural background predicts the likelihood and speed of retaliation after a foul in professional soccer. Retaliation by the victim reduces the probability of repeated fouls by the perpetrator, but only if the perpetrator is from a culture that values revenge. Formal sanctions (yellow cards) reduce both the likelihood of retaliation and repeated offenses, and are about three times as effective as informal retaliation in deterring repeated fouls. There is evidence of crowding out: when a foul is formally sanctioned, victims are less likely to retaliate. The study does not report or analyze any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that informal and formal sanctions can reduce repeated transgressions in a real-world setting, and that the effectiveness of informal sanctions depends on cultural context. However, because the setting is not a public goods game or a close variant, and because no payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, the paper offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task. It may inform mechanism discussions about the interaction of formal and informal punishment, and about heterogeneity in punishment effectiveness, but does not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bar-On, K. K., & Lamm, E. (2023). The interplay of social identity and norm psychology in the evolution of human groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper proposes that norm psychology (the internalization of social norms) and social identity theory (the internalization of group identity) are deeply intertwined processes that together shape human norm-following behaviour. It suggests that norm adherence is flexible and context-dependent, influenced by both personal commitment to norms and the desire to maintain social identity. Punishment of norm violators is discussed as a social process (e.g., gossip, humour) that reinforces group identity and norm compliance. However, the paper does not address efficiency, payoff, or public goods game outcomes, nor does it provide empirical evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not discuss game design dimensions, control or treatment efficiency, or economic outcomes. Its relevance is limited to high-level theoretical framing about the psychological and social mechanisms underlying norm enforcement and group behaviour. It may inform background understanding of why punishment and norm enforcement are important in human groups, but it does not provide actionable evidence for the downstream prediction task.
